
## honest/sections/preface.tex

## honest/sections/biases-an-introduction.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: defined base rate neglect, sunk cost fallacy and the bias blind spot; corrected the paintings study (subjects predicted the bias in advance); said what 'expertise' means in Serfas; said what the four sequences are about and what Korzybski's saying claims

- Before: That is base rate neglect. The sunk cost fallacy is another bias.
- After: That is base rate neglect: judging by how well traits seem to fit together, and ignoring how common each is. Another bias is the sunk cost fallacy, the pull to stay committed to what you have already spent resources on when you should cut your losses.

- Before: In one study, people judged paintings in a way they themselves rated as biased, were biased by it, and afterward claimed their judgments had been objective. People also see bias in others more easily than in themselves.
- After: In one study, people predicted that knowing a painting was by a famous artist would make it harder for them to judge it neutrally. When tested, they showed exactly that bias, and afterward they claimed their judgments had been objective. People also see bias in others more easily than in themselves: finding no ``biased-feeling thoughts'' when they look inward, they conclude that they are less biased than everyone else.

- Before: accounting courses reduced people's sunk-cost bias, while years of work experience did not.
- After: accounting courses reduced people's sunk-cost bias, while years of work experience did not. Serfas calls the difference expertise as opposed to experience: understanding why the bias arises, being able to spot it in a given setting, and having tools to counter it. The book aims at that kind of expertise about human bias and self-deception.

- Before: and its organizing idea is Alfred Korzybski's: ``The map is not the territory.'' Its four sequences follow, and then a dialogue, ``The Simple Truth.''
- After: and its organizing idea is Alfred Korzybski's: ``The map is not the territory.'' A correct map has a structure similar to the territory's, which is what makes it useful, and the same holds for beliefs and words. The four sequences take this up in turn: how our beliefs fail to map the world in systematic ways (``Predictably Wrong''), what makes a belief a map at all (``Fake Beliefs''), how the brain's mapping works (``Noticing Confusion''), and what happens when these points meet (``Mysterious Answers''). Then comes a dialogue about truth itself, ``The Simple Truth.''


## honest/sections/scope-insensitivity.tex

- Before: Surveys about Ontario's lakes and western wilderness areas found the same.
- After: Other surveys found the same. Toronto residents would pay little more to clean up all the polluted lakes in Ontario than those of one region, and residents of four western states would pay only 28\% more to protect all 57 wilderness areas in those states than to protect one.

- Before: and the feeling this image produces sets what they pay.
- After: and the feeling this image produces sets what they pay. The image is the same whatever the number, and no one can picture 2,000 birds, let alone 200,000, so the number is ``tossed out the window.''

- Before: that exponential increases in scope produce linear increases in payment.
- After: that exponential increases in scope produce linear increases in payment, as if the extra zeros added a small feeling to the image's feeling instead of multiplying it.

- Before: I call a fourfold rise insensitivity, and do not say what rise would have been right.
- After: I call a fourfold rise insensitivity, and do not say what rise would have been right. Baron and Greene found no effect at all when the number of lives saved changed by a factor of ten.

- Before: is my source for the claim that ``our perception of human deaths follows Weber's Law.''
- After: is my source for the claim that ``our perception of human deaths follows Weber's Law'': we notice a difference in deaths only when it is a fixed fraction of the total, so the same number of lives seems to matter less when the total is larger.


## honest/sections/the-martial-art-of-rationality.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: said what each new field contributes, what the AI comparison shows (compensating for machinery built to rationalize), and what applying the science to intuition means; removed a leftover 'I end'

- Before: In the last few decades we have learned a great deal about human reasoning: the research on heuristics and biases, Bayesian statistics, evolutionary psychology, social psychology.
- After: In the last few decades we have learned a great deal about human reasoning: the research on heuristics and biases, Bayesian statistics, evolutionary psychology, social psychology. Experiments find the errors, probability theory says what the experiments mean, and evolution explains why the errors are there.

- Before: In a human you cannot rewire the machinery, and some of the machinery is built to rationalize.
- After: The personal art means pulling the right levers at the right time on a large thinking machine you cannot open up. Some of it was built by selection pressures that run against our goals: we decide to seek only the truth, but the brain has ``hardwired support for rationalizing falsehoods.'' We cannot rewire it; we can only learn to compensate.

- Before: I end with three sentences of ``must.'' We must move our own mental limbs, we must connect theory to practice, and we must see what the science means for our daily inner life.
- After: So we must use the science to correct our intuitions and to improve how we watch our own thinking. It is our own mental limbs we have to move, so we must connect theory to practice and see what the science means for our daily inner life.

- Before: A 1979 follow-up found that the errors matched what two newspapers reported.
- After: A 1979 follow-up found that the errors correlated strongly, 0.85 and 0.89, with how often two newspapers reported each cause of death.

- Before: between them and an event. I give no source for that.
- After: between them and an event. I give no source for that. Today a report may pass through six bloggers before it reaches you, and far more happens in a far larger world, so far less of it reaches you: a stronger filter and a larger bias.

- Before: You hear about Bill Gates far more than his numbers justify, and you may compare your success to his and feel worse.
- After: You hear about Bill Gates far more than his numbers justify, and you may compare your success to his and feel worse. The reverse also holds: 19\% of the world lives on less than \$1 a day, and they do not write a fifth of the blog posts you read.

- Before: People refuse flood insurance even when it is heavily subsidized.
- After: When there has been no recent flood, people refuse flood insurance even when it is heavily subsidized and priced far below its fair value.

- After: Dams prevent small floods, so people take fewer precautions; floods become rarer but each does so much more damage that the average yearly damage rises.

- After: From floods I draw a rule. The wise would reason from small hazards to the possibility of large ones; instead, experience of small hazards sets a ceiling on perceived risk. A society protected against minor hazards ``takes no action against major risks'' and builds on flood plains; a society used to minor hazards guards against those and not against the rare large one.

## honest/sections/what-s-a-bias.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: stated the three possible origins of bias accurately, what the availability algorithm does, the colloquial sense excluded, and that mistakes may trace back to biases

- Before: The machinery might have evolved to win arguments, or to believe what others believe, or a useful shortcut might have side effects. The availability heuristic is my example of the last: it does good work but also makes systematic errors.
- After: The machinery might have evolved to believe some false things because that helped win political arguments in a tribe; or it might not care much about truth, as when we believe what others believe in order to get along; or a useful shortcut might have side effects. The availability heuristic is my example of the last. It gives more weight to what comes to mind more easily, which does good work but also makes systematic errors.

- Before: Once someone has identified such a problem ``verbally and concretely,'' we call it a bias.
- After: Once someone has identified such a problem ``verbally and concretely,'' we call it a bias. This is not the everyday sense, in which a biased person has a prejudiced attitude.

- Before: which come from learned false beliefs and are ``much easier to correct.''
- After: which come from learned false beliefs and are ``much easier to correct,'' though a mistake may itself trace back to a bias.


## honest/sections/burdensome-details.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: explained how representativeness produces the fallacy, stated the two forecasting statements and the factor-of-four penalty, described the dice experiment, gave the point of the futurist story and the China example; removed 'I close'

- Before: and a long series of experiments ``confirmed'' this. Detail makes a story more plausible and less probable.
- After: and a long series of experiments ``confirmed'' this. An added detail can make an outcome look more typical of what produces it: the unlikely claim and the likely one ``average out.'' So detail makes a story more plausible and less probable. We should then expect futurists to tell very plausible, detailed histories of the future, and people to accept large packages of unsupported claims wrapped around a few strong ones.

- Before: In the 1982 forecasting experiment each group saw one statement, and the one that included an invasion of Poland got the higher probability. What could they have done? It seems to me that they would need to notice the word ``and'' and be ``shocked'' by it. They might also have thought of all the reasons relations could be suspended.
- After: In a 1982 experiment, professional forecasters gave a higher probability to ``Russia invades Poland, followed by suspension of diplomatic relations between the USA and the USSR'' than to the suspension alone. Each group saw only one statement, so no one could compare them. What could they have done? It seems to me that they would need to notice the word ``and'' and be ``shocked'' by it, and cut the probability by a factor of four at least, as the results imply. They might also have thought of all the reasons relations could be suspended, since the question was about any reason, not this one.

- Before: The Reagan subjects should add absurdities, measured in bits, rather than average them: 1 bit plus 4 bits makes 5.
- After: The Reagan subjects should add absurdities, measured in bits (the logarithm of the probability), rather than average them: cutting support to local governments may or may not happen (1 bit), supporting unwed mothers is very unlikely (4 bits), total 5 bits. Or simply: one strike and it is out.

- Before: In the dice experiment the subjects should have thought: ``Aha! Sequence 1 is short!''
- After: In a 1983 experiment, subjects bet on which of three sequences would appear in twenty rolls of a die with four green faces and two red: RGRRR, GRGRRR or GRRRRR. 65\% chose GRGRRR, though any run containing it also contains RGRRR. They thought the sequence with the most green was best; they should have thought: ``Aha! Sequence 1 is short!''

- Before: He said ``Oh.'' So hold up each detail and ask ``How do we know this detail?''
- After: He said ``Oh.'' He had heard evidence that universes replicate and taken it as support for the whole package told around it. So hold up each detail and ask ``How do we know this detail?'' If someone describes a future nanotechnological war in which China breaks an international agreement, ask how they know it will be China.

- Before: I close, ``For it is written,'' with two lines about lightening your burden.
- After: The post ends, ``For it is written,'' with two lines: lighten your burden if you can, since any straw can break your back.


## honest/sections/what-do-we-mean-by-rationality-1.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: gave the bookcase example and the map-territory sense of truth, the 'true' analogy behind keeping the word, the definition of Bayesian, the two kinds of disputed puzzle, the fallback to 'truth' and 'winning' (fire example), reflective consistency, the point about communication, and the second example of specific language; removed 'I end'

- Before: I mean two things by rationality. ``Epistemic rationality'' is making your beliefs more accurate. ``Instrumental rationality'' is getting what you value, which I sometimes call ``winning.'' I give no source for the pair.
- After: I mean two things by rationality. ``Epistemic rationality'' is making your beliefs more accurate. You look around a room and place the bookcase against the wall; if your eyes or brain go wrong, your model shows a bookcase where there is none, and you are disappointed when you go for a book. A belief is a map, and its correspondence with the territory is what I call truth. ``Instrumental rationality'' is steering the future toward outcomes you rank higher, which I sometimes call ``winning.'' Winning does not mean winning at others' expense, since what you value includes other people. I give no source for the pair.

- Before: My answer is that we need it for general statements, and my example is that rational agents maximize expected utility.
- After: My answer is an analogy with ``true.'' We never need to say ``it is true that snow is white,'' but we need the word for general statements such as ``True models usually produce better experimental predictions than false models.'' In the same way, ``It's rational to eat vegetables'' just means useful, but ``Rational agents make decisions that maximize the probabilistic expectation of a coherent utility function'' needs the concept.

- Before: The ``gold standards'' are probability theory and decision theory, and someone who obeys them is ``acting like a Bayesian.''
- After: The ``gold standards'' are probability theory and decision theory. Probability theory governs belief the same way whether you are locating a bookcase or estimating the hairs on Julius Caesar's head, and decision theory governs action whatever your goals. Beliefs that fit a coherent probability distribution, and choices that maximize the expected value of a coherent utility function, are called Bayesian.

- Before: Second, the mathematics itself is disputed in some puzzles, such as Newcomb's problem.
- After: Second, the mathematics itself is disputed in some puzzles: the rules of probability, in problems where the number of observers is uncertain, and the rules of decision theory, in problems such as Newcomb's, where another agent predicts your decision before you make it. Where the formal tools fall short we can fall back on plain ``truth'' and ``winning.'' A scientist first studying fire does better to point at a campfire and say ``that orangey-bright hot stuff'' than to define fire by phlogiston, and a bias hits no less hard because we cannot define irrationality.

- Before: It is futile to settle a dispute by defining ``rational'' so that your answer wins.
- After: It is futile to settle a dispute by defining ``rational'' so that your answer wins; that only raises the question why anyone should accept your definition. I care about Bayesian updating, with a prior that favors simple hypotheses, not because Laplace handed it down but because I expect it to bring beliefs closer to accuracy. Words exist to carry thoughts from one mind to another. If you understand the concept I am getting at, the question of definition is finished, and what is left is what counts as a good way to think.

- Before: you are probably using the word to mean something else.
- After: you are probably using the word to mean something else: rationality should be consistent under reflection, so that looking rationally at the evidence and looking rationally at how your mind handles the evidence give the same answer.

- Before: As an example of plain and specific language, I offer this: causal decision theory
- After: Where people disagree about the word, use more specific language: ``The self-benefiting thing to do is to run away, but I hope I would at least try to drag the child off the railroad tracks.'' Or: causal decision theory

- Before: I end: do not overuse the word. ``One receives no points merely for pronouncing it loudly.''
- After: Do not overuse the word. ``One receives no points merely for pronouncing it loudly,'' and if you speak too much of the Way, you will not reach it.


## honest/sections/planning-fallacy.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: explained why realistic and best-case scenarios coincide, what the inside view is and why it tempts, and gave the Christmas-shopping numbers

- Before: Another study found that people's ``realistic'' scenarios were the same as their best-case ones.
- After: Another study found that people's ``realistic'' scenarios were the same as their best-case ones: asked for a realistic plan, people picture everything going as planned, with no unexpected delays.

- Before: The cure is the ``outside view'': ignore what is special about your project and ask how long similar projects took.
- After: Planning as we usually do it is the ``inside view'': work out where, when and how, estimate time and resources, and picture the steps from start to finish. It leaves out the delays no one foresees. The cure is the ``outside view'': ignore what is special about your project and ask how long broadly similar projects took. This feels wrong, since the inside view has more detail, but in experiments more detailed plans were more optimistic and less accurate.

- Before: Shoppers who planned their Christmas shopping in detail were more optimistic than those who did not.
- After: Shoppers asked to plan their Christmas shopping in detail expected to finish more than a week before Christmas; shoppers simply asked when they would finish said four days before. Both groups finished three days before.


## honest/sections/why-truth.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: stated the three factors in the value of information, why practical truth gives an outside check, how curiosity ranks problems, why explicit rules are still needed, and that all three motives are kept

- Before: Then you rank questions by how much the answer could change what you do. If the plane falls, or the shop has no chocolate milk, you learn that you thought badly.
- After: Then you rank questions by the expected value of the information: how much the possible answers affect your choices, how much the choices matter, and how likely you are to find an answer that changes what you would otherwise do. This may seem impure, but it gives an outside check: if the plane falls, or the shop has no chocolate milk, you learn that you thought badly, and so you learn which ways of thinking work.

- Before: Third, curiosity. It is pure, and ranks questions by how interesting they are.
- After: Third, curiosity. It is pure, and ranks questions by how interesting they are; a harder problem with more chance of failure may be worth more effort just because it is more fun.

- Before: To improve we need deliberate rules of thinking, ``things that look like norms of rationalist `propriety.'\,''
- After: To improve beyond the hunter-gatherer standard we need deliberate rules of thinking, ``things that look like norms of rationalist `propriety.'\,'' A new habit of thought starts as an explicit rule and only slowly, if ever, becomes part of how we are moved.

- Before: Last, asked which motive is most foundational, I answer ``curiosity.''
- After: All three, curiosity, practical use and these rules, belong to the project. Asked which is most foundational, I answer ``curiosity.''


## honest/sections/feeling-rational.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: named System 1 and System 2, made the test depend on conduct as the post does, spelled out the iron passage and the goblin argument, and what fleeing sadness means; removed 'I end'

- Before: I suspect such people are really contrasting fast perceptual judgments with slow deliberate ones, and each kind can be right or wrong.
- After: I can find no theorem of probability that says I should look ice-cold. I suspect such people are really contrasting ``System 1'' and ``System 2,'' fast perceptual judgments and slow deliberate ones, and each kind can serve the truth or defeat it.

- Before: I label an emotion ``not rational'' if it rests on mistaken beliefs, and rational if correct beliefs evoke it. I quote a passage about fearing a cool iron and staying calm before a hot one, without saying where it comes from.
- After: I label an emotion ``not rational'' if it rests on mistaken beliefs, or rather on habits of thought that produce mistakes, and rational if correct beliefs or sound thinking evoke it. I quote a passage without saying where it comes from: if an iron comes toward your face and you think it hot when it is cool, the Way opposes your fear; if you think it cool when it is hot, the Way opposes your calm.

- Before: If you believe a goblin tied your shoelaces together, that is a belief about the world. Anger at the goblin, I grant, is ``not just'' about the world.
- After: If you believe a goblin tied your shoelaces together, that is a belief about the world: anything that can tie real laces must be real too. Anger at the goblin, I grant, is ``not just'' about the world. A calm person would expect the same knotted laces, since what happens in the closet does not depend on your mood.

- Before: When something terrible happens, I do not flee my sadness.
- After: When something good happens, I am happy without wondering whether I may be. When something terrible happens, I do not flee my sadness by looking for ``fake consolations and false silver linings.''

- Before: I end: ``But I know, now, that it is rational to feel.''
- After: ``But I know, now, that it is rational to feel.''


## honest/sections/the-lens-that-sees-its-flaws.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: removed motives and mockery the post does not support (why the steps are described, 'in case the first marvel did not take'); stated the mouse argument, what science is on this account, what the chatroom answer shows, what the correction is, and what the footnote argues

- Before: Light leaves the Sun, bounces off your shoelaces, enters your eye, and becomes the belief that your shoelaces are untied. I describe every step of this, because it sounds like physics and makes what follows sound like physics too.

My point is that you can understand this process and a mouse cannot. I ask you to marvel at it. Then I tell you about the mouse again, and ask you to marvel again, in case the first marvel did not take.

Science is this same understanding made systematic.
- After: Light leaves the Sun, bounces off your shoelaces, enters your eye, triggers nerve impulses, and is rebuilt in the brain as a model of an untied shoelace; so you believe your shoelaces are untied. None of this is magic, and you can understand it, and so think about which ways of thinking make beliefs mirror reality and which do not.

A mouse can see but cannot understand seeing. Its world holds cats, holes and cheese but not mouse brains, so it cannot correct for an optical illusion: its camera takes no pictures of its own lens. We can look at a strange image and realize that part of what we see is the lens. You need not always believe your eyes, but you must know that you have eyes, and keep separate places in your mind for the map and the territory. I ask you to marvel at this, since it is rare among animals.

Science is this same understanding made systematic: reasoning about a more reliable process for making the mind mirror the world. Thinking about ``performing replicable experiments to falsify theories,'' we can see why it works. It is not a separate realm for laboratories but an understandable process that ties brains to reality.

- Before: From those two words I work out his unhappiness and what his brain is doing.
- After: From those two words I work out that the thought of nuclear war made him unhappy, and that his brain therefore rejected it.

- Before: The principle is that wanting something to be true is not evidence that it is true.
- After: Across a billion such worlds, his way of thinking would not make optimists more common in the worlds without war. The principle is that wanting something to be true is not evidence that it is true. Asking which beliefs make you happy looks inward and tells you about yourself; happiness should follow from your picture of the world, not shape it.

- Before: I conclude that a mind which knows its own flaws can apply a reflective correction, and that this makes it ``far more powerful.'' I do not say what the correction is, or how you would know it had worked.
- After: I conclude that a mind which knows its own flaws can apply a reflective correction: if you see that hope is shifting your beliefs too far, you can correct for it. A lens that understands its own systematic errors is not perfect but ``far more powerful.'' I do not say how you would know the correction had worked.

- Before: In a footnote I say that a small effect should move your beliefs only a little, and that we must now bring in Bayes's theorem and measure it. Then I measure nothing.
- After: A footnote answers a clever objection: hope makes me work harder, which helps the economy and so makes war less likely. We must bring in Bayes's theorem and measure the effect, and your optimism cannot lower the chance of nuclear war by 20\%, or by however much it moved your belief. I give no other number.


## honest/sections/making-beliefs-pay-rent.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: replaced 'I do not yet say how' (the post does say how: compare what the two expect), stated the bowling-ball point correctly, said how phlogiston failed, the quiz point, what a null answer means, and the closing rule; removed 'I end'

- Before: The foundational skill of rationality ``might be'' the ability to spot in your own head whether you have a map of something or not. I do not yet say how.

Would the two arguers expect to see the tree fallen in different places, or hear anything different on a recorder? No.
- After: The foundational skill of rationality ``might be'' the ability to spot in your own head whether you have a map of something or not. The test is to ask what each side expects to experience. Would the two arguers expect to see the tree fallen in different places, hear anything different on a recorder left beside it, or see a different trace on a brain monitor? No.

- Before: But atoms and floors are real, though we never sense them directly.
- After: But atoms and floors are real, though we never sense them directly: we see light from the floor, processed by the eye and brain, and inferring the floor is a step back to an unseen cause, however short.

- Before: Together they predict a crash five seconds later. So beliefs make predictions in combination.
- After: These are sentences, not anticipations of any sensation, but together they predict one: if the second hand is on the 12 when you drop the ball, you expect it on the 1 when you hear the crash. To anticipate experience precisely, we must use beliefs that are not themselves anticipations.

- Before: and the belief predicted nothing in advance.
- After: and the belief predicted nothing in advance: the link from phlogiston to the fire was always drawn after the fire, never before.

- Before: ``Nothing,'' I answer. I made up both terms.
- After: ``Nothing,'' I answer. But you had better remember that he has both attributes, so you can repeat them on the quiz. I made up both terms.

- Before: My example of a belief that forbids nothing is élan vital, a life force that nobody believes in any more.
- After: My example of a belief that forbids nothing is élan vital, a life force that nobody believes in any more. If you cannot name anything a belief rules out, it permits anything to happen to you, and it floats.

- Before: I end: ``Every question of belief should flow from a question of anticipation.''
- After: Above all, do not ask what to believe; ask what to anticipate. ``Every question of belief should flow from a question of anticipation.''

- After: Eddin, a Green, laughs at a history of murder over ``one pointless thing,'' and wonders how to keep the news from blowing up the world, and whether they all deserve it.

## honest/sections/belief-in-belief.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: stated the post's picture of two kinds of belief side by side, why empiricism alone is not the cure, the blue-and-green sky example, the logic analogy, and the post's final formulation of what the claimant does

- Before: ``As if the human brain only had enough disk space to represent one belief at a time!''
- After: ``As if the human brain only had enough disk space to represent one belief at a time!'' The claimant does not anticipate seeing a dragon, or there would be no advance excuses; the sentence ``There is a dragon in my garage'' may still sit among the claimant's verbal beliefs. The two ought to clash, but you can write ``The sky is green!'' next to a picture of a blue sky and the paper does not burst into flames.

- Before: Asking what a belief predicts will not cure this, I admit, because the dragon belief connects easily to what one would see in the garage.
- After: Asking what a belief predicts will not cure this, I admit. It is easy to connect the dragon to experience: if there is a dragon, you expect to see one when you open the door, and if you see none, there is none. The invisibility is a symptom of something worse.

- Before: Daniel Dennett calls the real problem ``belief in belief'': it is often easier to believe that you ought to believe something than to believe it.
- After: Daniel Dennett calls the real problem ``belief in belief'': it is often easier to believe that you ought to believe something than to believe it. It is not even clear what you would believe if you believed that the Ultimate Cosmic Sky is perfectly blue and perfectly green, but it is easy to believe that believing it is good and virtuous.

- Before: ``Nobody will admit to themselves'' otherwise, unless unusually honest.
- After: ``Nobody will admit to themselves'' otherwise, unless unusually honest: people do not believe in belief in belief, they just believe in belief. (Mathematical logic trains you to keep P, a proof of P, and a proof that P is provable apart; the same sharp lines separate P, wanting P, believing P, wanting to believe P, and believing that you believe P.)

- Before: You can expect no dragon when you open your garage without ever thinking the sentence ``There is no dragon in my garage.''
- After: You can expect no dragon when you open your garage without ever thinking the sentence ``There is no dragon in my garage,'' and you can flinch from admitting you do not believe without ever thinking ``I want to believe.'' So it is not realistic to say the claimant believes it is beneficial to believe in the dragon; it is realistic to say the claimant ``anticipates as if'' there is no dragon and ``makes excuses as if they believed in the belief.''


## honest/sections/religion-s-claim-to-be-non-disprovable.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: showed why Elijah's contest reads as an experiment, and added the Ark seekers, Sinai as evidence, the Egypt-ruled-Canaan point, the Bayesian reading of the exorcism, the list of fields religion lost, why Bible ethics escape notice, and the bloody-axe analogy

- Before: The earliest account I know of a scientific experiment is Elijah's contest with the priests of Baal: each side lays out a bull, and the true God is to send fire. It falls on Elijah's altar,
- After: The earliest account I know of a scientific experiment is Elijah's contest with the priests of Baal: each side lays out a bull on the same kind of wood, and the true God is to send fire. The priests of Baal are the control group, and Elijah soaks his own altar with water, accepting the burden of proof. The fire falls on Elijah's altar,

- Before: In the old days people believed their religions as fact.
- After: In the old days people believed their religions as fact. The archaeologists who searched for Noah's Ark expected they might become famous.

- Before: and separating religion from fact is ``a recent and strictly Western concept.''
- After: and separating religion from fact is ``a recent and strictly Western concept.'' Orthodox Judaism holds that God spoke at Mount Sinai in a thundering voice; if that happened, it would be plain evidence of some superhuman being, though not that it was God, or good.

- Before: With no libraries to check it, the Old Testament could claim the Exodus, of which Egyptologists found no trace.
- After: With no libraries to check it, the Old Testament could claim the Exodus, of which Egyptologists found no trace. They did find that Egypt ruled much of Canaan at the supposed time, a large error that no one could then catch.

- Before: like casting out the spirit that made a boy froth at the mouth.
- After: like casting out the spirit that made a boy froth at the mouth. That is still ordinary evidence: if epilepsy is caused by demons, and demons flee a true prophet but not a fraud, then the end of the fit shows Jesus is a true prophet.

- Before: Religion once made claims about everything, and ethics is what is left.
- After: Religion once made claims about everything: law before legislatures, history before historians, sexual morals, forms of government, and science from the classing of animals to the formation of stars. Better institutions took over each field, and ethics is what is left.

- Before: I add, with no evidence: ``Most people's concept of rationality is determined by what they think they can get away with.''
- After: If you say the Earth is flat, people think you mad; if you say the Bible is your source of ethics, they do not. I add, with no evidence: ``Most people's concept of rationality is determined by what they think they can get away with,'' and everyone has agreed not to notice.

- Before: false to history, to scripture, to what children are told and to what most believers hold.
- After: false to history, to scripture, to what children are told and to what most believers hold. It is like a defendant who, faced with the bloody axe, says his innocence cannot be disproved by mere evidence, since it is a separate magisterium.

- After: A ``proper'' belief controls what you anticipate; it can still be wrong, as when someone expects prayer to cure a sick baby. The kinds I described earlier, belief in belief, professing and cheering, are ``improper,'' arguably not belief at all. Here is a fourth:

## honest/sections/pretending-to-be-wise.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: gave the question the parents deflected, what the principal should have said, the revered elder, Rooney's point, why neutrality is attackable, and Hanson's 'pull the rope sideways'

- Before: My parents answered my theological questions by saying
- After: My parents answered theological questions, such as why ancient Egypt, which kept good records, has none of Jews ever being there, by saying

- Before: Of course it matters; to adults a fight is only inconvenient.
- After: Of course it matters. If the principal does not know who started it, the principal should say so; let a parent punch the principal and see how far ``it doesn't matter who started it'' gets with a judge. But to adults a fight is only inconvenient, and it only matters that it end quickly.

- Before: The rest is status: a principal who took sides would become ``a mere participant in the fray.''
- After: The rest is status: a principal who took sides would become ``a mere participant in the fray.'' So too the revered elder, a chief executive, a famous academic or the founder of a mailing list, who is respected as a judge on condition of almost never judging.

- Before: Suspending judgment is sometimes rational, as a commenter, Michael Rooney, said.
- After: Suspending judgment is sometimes rational. A commenter, Michael Rooney, noted a related error: beginning philosophy students, given reasons for doubt, conclude not that they should suspend judgment but that every view is as plausible as any other.

- Before: ``Neutrality is a definite judgment.'' It can be wrong.
- After: ``Neutrality is a definite judgment.'' It says the evidence supports only a neutral summing-up; it must predict something, it can be wrong, and it is as open to attack as any side.

- Before: Robin Hanson says divisive conversations are a limited resource, so you may spend yours elsewhere.
- After: Robin Hanson says divisive conversations are a limited resource, so you may spend yours on less common questions where you can ``pull the rope sideways'' and a little discussion does more good.


## honest/sections/applause-lights.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: said what the question was meant to find out, why democracy is a conflict-resolution mechanism, and what the imagined talk sounds like; removed 'I end'

- Before: or rebel nerds whose AI polls the whole world and does what the majority says.
- After: or rebel nerds whose AI polls the whole world and does what the majority says. I wanted to learn whether he valued democracy for how well it works or for the moral rightness of voting.

- Before: The substance of a democracy is a mechanism for resolving policy conflicts.
- After: The substance of a democracy is a mechanism for resolving policy conflicts; if everyone wanted the same policies, no democracy would be needed. The mechanism can be a majority vote, an elected legislature or even an AI that responds to voters, but it has to be something.

- Before: I end with a talk I am tempted to give, made of nothing but applause lights, to see how long the audience takes to start laughing.
- After: Last, a talk I am tempted to give, made of nothing but applause lights, to see how long the audience takes to start laughing: we must balance the risks and opportunities, plan wisely and rationally, avoid both fear and blind enthusiasm, respect all stakeholders, share the benefits widely, and think before it is too late.

## honest/sections/what-is-evidence.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added the rock-as-witness point, the retina analogy behind 'blind faith', the phone example, and the closing argument in full; removed 'I end'

- Before: Light from your shoelaces that hits a rock leaves no trace in the rock; a photograph or a working eye keeps one.
- After: Light from your shoelaces that hits a rock leaves no trace in the rock, which is why rocks make poor witnesses in court; a photograph or a working eye keeps one, and if your eyes and brain work, you become tied to your shoelaces.

- Before: Some belief systems, ``in a rather obvious trick,'' say that certain beliefs must be held no matter what. I do not name the belief systems or quote any of them.
- After: If your retina ended up in the same state whatever light entered it, you would be blind. Some belief systems, ``in a rather obvious trick,'' say that certain beliefs must be held no matter what you see; hence ``blind faith.'' A belief that does not depend on what you see blinds you as surely as losing your eyes. I do not name the belief systems or quote any of them.

- Before: Your beliefs, once spoken, can be evidence for someone else.
- After: Your beliefs, once spoken, can be evidence for someone else, since speaking and hearing are causes too: saying ``My shoelaces are untied'' over the phone shares your tie to your shoelaces with a friend.

- Before: I end by asking you to explain why your way of thinking produces true beliefs.
- After: So try to explain why your way of thinking produces beliefs that mirror reality: why minds that think as you do will believe ``snow is white'' if and only if snow is white. If you do not believe your thinking is tied to reality, why believe what it tells you?


## honest/sections/scientific-evidence-legal-evidence-rational-evidence.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: gave the full corruption argument, what counts as legal evidence, why science adds standards, the 'prediction #27' example, the p-value example, and the reasons for open access

- Before: My explanation is that if we jailed everyone the commissioner named, corruption would grow, ``like honey attracts flies.''
- After: My explanation: if we jailed everyone the commissioner named, we might at first catch many crime bosses, but unchecked power attracts corruption ``like honey attracts flies,'' so over time real bosses would hide better and more innocent people would be jailed.

- Before: So legal evidence is a subset of rational evidence.
- After: So, for a social goal, we count as legal evidence only certain kinds, such as the commissioner's own observations on a given night. Legal evidence should all be rational evidence, but not the other way round.

- Before: Science is made of generalizations that let you
- After: Science too is a social process run by fallible people, and it wants a protected pool of especially reliable beliefs, so it adds its own strong standards. It is made of generalizations that let you

- Before: Then I suggest that it is not: if you already knew the scientific belief about an experiment's result, ``why bother to run the experiment?''
- After: Then I suggest that it is not. Suppose you test an accepted theory's twenty-seventh prediction in a new setting, with no reason to doubt it. Before the test there is ``theory Q's prediction,'' but it seems dangerous to call it a scientific belief: if you already knew the scientific belief about the result, ``why bother to run the experiment?'' That is why I tie science to generalizations, which can be tested again, and not to single events, which cannot.

- Before: ``That is not a well-formed question'': the standards are pragmatic choices.
- After: ``That is not a well-formed question'': the standards are pragmatic choices. Nothing written on the stars makes p < 0.05 the standard for publication, and many argue it should be 0.01 or 0.001.

- Before: Perhaps, I suggest, future generations will count as scientific only papers published in open-access journals, and I think that would serve science better.
- After: Perhaps future generations will count as scientific only papers published in open-access journals: if you charge for knowledge, is it the knowledge of humankind, and can we fully trust a result that people must pay to criticize? I think that rule would serve science better.


## honest/sections/how-much-evidence-does-it-take.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: named the likelihood ratio and showed the odds calculation, the lottery's setup, the arithmetic from 28 to 34 bits, and the fuel analogy's point

- Before: I start with a lottery: 131,115,985 possible tickets, one winner, all equally likely.
- After: I start with a lottery: seventy balls, six to match, so 131,115,985 possible tickets, one winner, all equally likely.

- Before: A black box always beeps for the winning ticket, and beeps a quarter of the time for a losing one. Tried on every ticket, it beeps for about 33 million losers.
- After: A black box always beeps for the winning ticket, and beeps a quarter of the time for a losing one: a ``likelihood ratio'' of 4 to 1. Tried on every ticket, it beeps for about 33 million losers. That does not win the lottery, but it improves your odds from 1 in 131 million to 1 in 33 million.

- Before: Evidence 131 million times more likely for the winner than for a loser leaves you with the winner and, on average, one loser, so an even chance.
- After: Evidence 131 million times more likely for the winner than for a loser leaves you with the winner and, on average, one loser, so only an even chance. The clean way to see it is in odds: start at 1 to 131,115,984, and each box multiplies the odds by 4.

- Before: Twenty-eight bits, or 14 boxes, bring you to odds of 2 to 1. Thirty-four bits make you 99\% sure.
- After: Since 131,115,984 is a little under 2 to the 27th power, twenty-eight bits, or 14 boxes, bring you to odds of 2 to 1. Each further box quadruples the odds, and thirty-four bits make you 99\% sure.

- Before: Believing without enough evidence is like driving a car without fuel.
- After: Believing without enough evidence is like driving a car without fuel. You can shut your eyes and pretend it is moving, but accurate belief needs evidence, and the further you want to go, the more you need.

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added the second half of the 'not criticizing' remark, the million-to-one example, and the contrast with the Traditional picture of hunch-then-confirmation

- Before: and I try not to criticize people when they are right.
- After: and I try not to criticize people when they are right: if they deserve criticism, an occasion when they are wrong will come soon enough.

- Before: To pick the correct hypothesis out of 100 million, you need about 27 bits of evidence. The evidence is needed before any justifying,
- After: To pick the correct hypothesis out of 100 million, you need about 27 bits of evidence; a test with a one-in-a-million false-positive rate, about 20 bits, would leave a hundred candidates. Traditional Rationality pictures a hunch that comes first and evidence gathered afterwards to justify it to others. On the Bayesian view the evidence is needed before any justifying,

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added that 'fitting' by not prohibiting is not enough, why a label does not lower complexity, why fit is a conserved probability, what goes wrong if either fit or complexity is ignored, and the point about compression

- Before: So the length of an English sentence is a poor measure. Words are labels for things the listener already knows, and a short label, ``Fnord!'', does not make a claim simple.
- After: So the length of an English sentence is a poor measure, and ``fitting'' the facts by merely not forbidding them is not enough. Words are labels for things the listener already knows, and a short label, ``Fnord!'', does not make a claim simple, since you must first tell the listener what it stands for.

- Before: In the version I prefer, programs assign probabilities to data. A program fixed to show HTTHHT fits that sequence 64 times better than a fair coin,
- After: In the version I prefer, programs assign probabilities to data, and fit means giving the observed data high probability. Probabilities sum to 1, so a program can fit one outcome better only by fitting others worse; there is no coin that gives heads and tails 100\% each. Judge by fit alone and you always prefer a program that predicts the exact data: one fixed to show HTTHHT fits that sequence 64 times better than a fair coin. Judge by simplicity alone and the fair coin always wins, even for HTHHTHHHTHHHHT..., where a slightly more complex hypothesis fits far better. So

- Before: or ``you will sooner or later decide that all fair coins are fixed.''
- After: or ``you will sooner or later decide that all fair coins are fixed.'' Moving a bit from the data into the program gains nothing unless the program compresses the data.

## honest/sections/your-strength-as-a-rationalist.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added the author's own chest-pain case, the 'felt a little forced' signal that the whole post turns on, the point that belief is easier than disbelief, and the 'zero knowledge' line; removed 'I end'

- Before: I also remembered that my own doctor never panicked about my symptoms, and was always right. So I told the stranger that if the paramedics said it was nothing, ``it must really be nothing.''
- After: I also remembered that my own doctor never panicked about my symptoms, and was always right; once, when I had chest pains, the doctor explained that I was describing muscle pain, not a heart attack. So I told the stranger that if the paramedics said it was nothing, ``it must really be nothing.'' I had explained the story within my model, though the fit ``still felt a little forced.''

- Before: Perhaps I should have realized that a friend of a stranger in a chatroom is less reliable than a published journal article.
- After: Perhaps I should have realized that a friend of a stranger in a chatroom is less reliable than a published journal article. But belief is easier than disbelief: we believe by instinct, and disbelief takes effort. I had all the information I needed, I even noticed the problem, and I ignored it.

- Before: Then the thesis: ``Your strength as a rationalist is your ability to be more confused by fiction than by reality.''
- After: Then the thesis: ``Your strength as a rationalist is your ability to be more confused by fiction than by reality.'' If you can explain any outcome equally well, you know nothing.

- Before: I end by wishing that the feeling of a forced explanation came
- After: That feeling of a forced fit is one of the most important a truthseeker can have. I wish it came


## honest/sections/absence-of-evidence-is-evidence-of-absence.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added that evidence is a matter of relative likelihood, why the theorem holds, the logic contrast, the full fossil-record point and the Fermi paradox; removed 'I end'

- Before: A secret Fifth Column might delay its sabotage,
- After: Evidence shifts probability toward the hypotheses that made it more likely, relative to the others; a hypothesis can give the evidence a high likelihood and still lose ground to one that gives it a higher one. A secret Fifth Column might delay its sabotage,

- Before: Then the notation, and the theorem: if seeing something would raise the probability of a hypothesis, then not seeing it must lower it.
- After: Then the notation, and the theorem: if seeing something would raise the probability of a hypothesis, then not seeing it must lower it, since the probability before looking is a weighted average of the two possible probabilities after, and so lies between them.

- Before: I grant that absence of proof is not proof of absence.
- After: I grant that absence of proof is not proof of absence: in logic, ``A implies B'' does not give ``not-A implies not-B.''

- Before: Gaps in the fossil record are very weak evidence, because fossils rarely form.
- After: Gaps in the fossil record are very weak evidence, because fossils rarely form, and it is futile to point to them when many strong positive finds are on record. If there are no positive observations at all, though, it is time to worry; hence the Fermi paradox, the absence of any sign of aliens.

- Before: I end by repeating a lesson from ``Your Strength as a Rationalist'': a model's strength lies in what it cannot explain.
- After: I repeat the lesson of ``Your Strength as a Rationalist'': a model's strength lies in what it cannot explain. If you do not notice when your model makes the evidence unlikely, you might as well have no model and no evidence.


## honest/sections/conservation-of-expected-evidence.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added the flight branch and why a single case hides the pattern, that expecting evidence should not move belief, the zero-sum point and 'test, not confirm'; removed a reporting tag

- Before: Fear in prison proved guilt, and so did calm. Spee heard many confessions, so he could see every branch: whatever the woman did, it counted against her.
- After: Fear in prison proved guilt, and so did calm. If she fled when accused, that proved guilt; if she stayed, the devil had held her there. Spee heard many confessions, so he could see every branch: whatever the woman did, it counted against her. In any one case you would hear only one branch.

- Before: I end the paragraph: ``On average, you must expect to be exactly as confident as when you started out.''
- After: ``On average, you must expect to be exactly as confident as when you started out.'' So merely expecting to see evidence, before you see it, should not move your belief.

- Before: ``For a true Bayesian, it is impossible to seek evidence that confirms a theory.''
- After: ``For a true Bayesian, it is impossible to seek evidence that confirms a theory''; you can only seek evidence that tests it.

- Before: since you need not plan how to make the evidence confirm your theory.
- After: since you need not plan how to make the evidence confirm your theory. Weakening what a surprising result would count against you can only be done by weakening, exactly as much, what an expected result would count for you; it is zero-sum.


## honest/sections/hindsight-devalues-science.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: named the textbook, defined hindsight bias, gave all five findings with their explanations, Baratz's example pair, and the two costs of hindsight

- Before: This essay is closely based on an excerpt from a social psychology textbook.
- After: This essay is closely based on an excerpt from Meyers's textbook \textsc{Exploring Social Psychology}.

- Before: That expectation ``is all hindsight.''
- After: That expectation ``is all hindsight.'' In hindsight bias, people who know the answer to a question say they would have given it a much higher probability than people who must guess without knowing.

- Before: I give five of their findings, each with a plausible explanation: better-educated soldiers adjusted worse, Southern soldiers coped better with the tropical heat, and so on. How many could you have predicted?
- After: I give five of their findings, each with a plausible explanation. Better-educated soldiers adjusted worse (intellectuals were less ready for battle). Southern soldiers coped better with the tropical heat (they were used to it). White privates were more eager for promotion than Black privates (oppression takes a toll on ambition). Southern Black soldiers preferred Southern white officers (who had more experience with Black men). Soldiers were more eager to go home while the fighting went on than after it ended (they were in danger). How many could you have predicted?

- Before: Daphna Baratz gave students pairs of findings, one true and one its opposite,
- After: Daphna Baratz gave students pairs of findings, one true and one its opposite, such as ``In prosperous times people spend a larger portion of their income than during a recession,''

- Before: I give no evidence for this.
- After: I give no evidence for this. The cost is that we undervalue the researchers' work and, worse, fail to notice evidence that does not fit what we really would have expected.


## honest/sections/illusion-of-transparency-why-no-one-understands-you.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: explained the mechanism, what the 'concealed' condition shows, the fuller bicycle numbers and the overhearers' 56%; removed 'I end'

- Before: I call this the illusion of transparency, and I report several studies.
- After: I call this the illusion of transparency. Rereading our own words, we find the meaning we intended falls into place, and it is hard to put ourselves in the place of someone who has only the words. It is close to hindsight bias: once we know, we cannot un-know. I report several studies.

- Before: Subjects told that the meal was bad but that Mark wanted to hide it thought June would not hear sarcasm in the same message.
- After: Subjects told that the meal was bad but that Mark wanted to hide it thought June would not hear sarcasm in the same message. As Keysar and Barr put it, it was as if June would perceive ``whatever intention Mark wanted her to perceive.''

- Before: They thought they were understood in 72\% of cases and were understood in 61\%.
- After: They thought they were understood in 72\% of cases and were understood in 61\%. When the listener had not understood, speakers thought they had in 46\% of cases; when the listener had understood, speakers thought not in only 12\%.

- Before: The same study had a second finding: people who overheard the speakers did not overestimate.
- After: The same study had a second finding: people who overheard the speakers did not overestimate, expecting listeners to understand in only 56\% of cases.

- Before: I end: ``Be not too quick to blame those who misunderstand your perfectly clear sentences.''
- After: ``Be not too quick to blame those who misunderstand your perfectly clear sentences'': your words are probably more ambiguous than you think.

- After: none says ``I don't know'' or ``This seems impossible.''

- After: Take the student who stammers, ``Eh, maybe because of the heat conduction and so?'' The words are easy to say loudly; do they control what the student expects?

## honest/sections/guessing-the-teacher-s-password.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: explained why school makes words seem to carry truth, corrected the 'wakalixes' sentence (the post's point is that with no teacher you may accept it), added the objection the post answers and the explanation/password distinction

- Before: Words have no intrinsic definitions; the syllables ``made of waves'' are not a hypothesis, only verbal behavior.
- After: Words have no intrinsic definitions: if I hear ``beaver'' and think of a large rodent, that is a fact about my mind, not about the syllables. ``Made of waves'' can call up a hypothesis in someone's mind, but the syllables themselves are neither right nor wrong. School gives gold stars for saying them, so students come to think that what they say has a truth-value of its own.

- Before: This is ``an attempt to guess the teacher's password.''
- After: This is not a hypothesis about the plate; it is ``an attempt to guess the teacher's password.'' Unlearning the habit starts with noticing the difference between an explanation and a password.

- Before: I call a wrong password ``wakalixes.''
- After: Is this too harsh? Can't ``heat conduction?'' be a first step? Maybe, if you do not think you are hunting for a password. But with no teacher to tell you that you failed, you may think ``Light is wakalixes'' a good explanation.

## honest/sections/fake-causality.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: said how phlogiston explained each observation, why the mind does not flag a fitted link, the looping-evidence example, the wrong way to count soldiers, and why a fake node feels like a fact

- Before: It explained flames, ash, flames going out in closed containers, and why charcoal leaves little residue.
- After: Phlogiston escaped from burning things as visible fire, leaving ash, the ``true material''; flames in closed containers went out because the air was saturated with phlogiston; charcoal left little residue because it was nearly pure phlogiston.

- Before: Phlogiston makes fire hot and bright, and that feels like an explanation too.
- After: Phlogiston makes fire hot and bright, and that feels like an explanation too, because the mind stores it in the same form. It does not notice when a cause's arrow constrains nothing, and hindsight makes a cause fitted to its effect feel as if it predicted it.

- Before: One of the main inspirations for Bayesian networks was the problem of evidence being counted twice as inference passes back and forth between cause and effect. Judea Pearl's example is soldiers counting their line: each passes back
- After: One of the main inspirations for Bayesian networks was the problem of evidence being counted twice as inference passes back and forth between cause and effect. A weak report that the sidewalk is wet makes rain likelier, which makes a wet sidewalk likelier, which makes it slippery, which makes it wet, which makes rain likelier again. Judea Pearl's example is soldiers counting their line. If you see the soldiers on each side of you and count three, then ask the one behind how many he sees and add his three, you get six, which is wrong. Instead each soldier passes back

- Before: Humans do not keep the two messages apart.
- After: Humans do not keep the two messages apart. We remember that phlogiston is hot and makes fire hot, so the theory seems to predict hot fire. Until you notice that it makes no advance predictions, the node is stored like any other fact.


## honest/sections/semantic-stopsigns.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added the regress through simulation, why 'God is uncaused' leaves the puzzle where it was, the hurricane example, the actual policy questions, and the warning against 'it's just a stopsign' as a counterargument; removed 'I end'

- Before: Science traces events back to the Big Bang, but the physical laws still seem to call for an explanation.
- After: Science traces events back to the Big Bang, but the physical laws still seem to call for an explanation; if we are a computer simulation, the computer runs on some other world's laws, and where did those come from?

- Before: If God is uncaused, we can still ask
- After: Saying that God is uncaused, or created Himself, leaves us just where ``Time began with the Big Bang'' did: we can still ask why the whole system exists, or

- Before: My answer is that saying ``God!'' ``is a way of belonging to a tribe.'' I give no evidence for this.
- After: My answer is that saying ``God!'' ``is a way of belonging to a tribe,'' so people say it as often as they can, even about why a hurricane struck New Orleans. I give no evidence for this. But even if God exists, saying so leaves the First Cause as puzzling as before; how could anyone not notice?

- Before: New technologies raise hard policy questions, and for any proposed institution the next question is whether it will work.
- After: New technologies raise hard policy questions: should a government supervise parents' choice of their child's genes, could parents choose genes for schizophrenia, should governments pay for intelligence enhancement to prevent a cognitive elite? For any proposed institution, charities or product-liability lawsuits for example, the next question is whether it will work.

- Before: I end with a caution. No word is a stopsign in itself;
- After: A caution: do not turn ``it's just a stopsign'' into a new all-purpose counterargument. No word is a stopsign in itself;


## honest/sections/mysterious-answers-to-mysterious-questions.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added the second Kelvin passage, why a mystery-substance explains nothing, the psychological tendency to posit substances over processes, the deeper error that an answer can be mysterious, and what 'no moving parts' means

- Before: In the third passage, from 1903,
- After: In the second, consciousness tells us that we direct forces on particles inside our bodies. In the third, from 1903,

- Before: Vitalism and phlogiston put a mystery into a substance, as the physician in Molière explains a sleeping draught by its ``dormitive potency.''
- After: Vitalism and phlogiston put a mystery into a substance, as the physician in Molière explains a sleeping draught by its ``dormitive potency.'' Neither made any outcome easier to explain than another; each wrapped the question in ``a small, hard, opaque black ball.'' Faced with a mystery, we more readily posit a mysterious substance than a complex process underneath. The deeper error is to think an answer can be mysterious: the vitalists saw a gap in their knowledge and plugged it with mysterious stuff, confusing the map with the territory. ``Mystery is a property of questions, not answers.''

- Before: it has no moving parts;
- After: it has no moving parts, being a blank substance or force whose workings are never spelled out;


## honest/sections/the-futility-of-emergence.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added the market-crash and 'arisence' illustrations, examples of legitimate 'arises from', how 'intelligence is emergent' meets each sign of a mysterious answer, and the closing point; removed 'I conclude'

- Before: Taken literally, that fits everything above the level of quarks.
- After: Taken literally, that fits everything above the level of quarks. Pointing at a market crash and saying ``It's not a quark!'' explains nothing, and neither does ``It's an emergent phenomenon!''

- Before: I object to the noun, not the verb. ``X emerges from Y'' is fine if Y is a specific model.
- After: I object to the noun, not the verb. ``X emerges from Y'' is fine if Y is a specific model, as gravity arises from the curvature of spacetime in general relativity, or chemistry from atoms in quantum electrodynamics. Saying that gravity depends on ``arisence'' would be no explanation.

- Before: I quote no one. Those who say it ``confess their ignorance of the internals, and take pride in it.''
- After: I quote no one. The phrase has every sign of a mysterious answer: it makes no new prediction about real minds, it satisfies curiosity without feeding it, it has no moving parts, those who say it ``confess their ignorance of the internals, and take pride in it,'' and intelligence stays as mysterious as before.

- Before: Humans are still humans, I conclude, even after a few science classes in college.
- After: Humans are still humans even after a few science classes in college: freed from settled science, they do what their ancestors did, dressed in the literary genre of science.


## honest/sections/say-not-complexity.tex

Why: Fidelity/substance pass: added what the Rubik's Cube question was really asking, why the author could not put the objection into words at once, the legitimate uses of complexity, the academic incentive, and why 'magic' is the better placeholder

- Before: I asked him how an AI might work out for itself how to solve a Rubik's Cube.
- After: I asked him how an AI might work out for itself how to solve a Rubik's Cube: not by preprogramming, but by discovering the cube's laws and inventing the idea of an ``operator,'' a reusable sequence of moves, which is the key to solving it.

- Before: He answered, ``But there's got to be some amount of complexity that does it.''
- After: He answered, ``But there's got to be some amount of complexity that does it.'' Saying ``complexity'' simply felt to me like the wrong move, and it took a moment to find words for why, since we cannot deliberate in words over every step of our thinking.

- Before: Complexity is a useful idea in its place, I concede, with proper mathematical definitions.
- After: Complexity is a useful idea in its place, I concede, with proper mathematical definitions, and even informally you must judge whether a hypothesis is too complicated for its evidence, or how to simplify a design.

- Before: The pressure to hide gaps, I suspect, is huge in academia
- After: The pressure to hide gaps, I suspect, is huge in academia, where a seemingly complete model with some ``emergent phenomena'' earns more credit than a map labelled ``then a miracle occurs,'' which a journal may not even accept. It is

- Before: Marcello and I agreed to say ``magic'' for whatever we did not understand, as a reminder that the problem was still unsolved.
- After: If you want examples of the skip-over, watch people discuss religion, philosophy or any science they were not trained in. Marcello and I agreed to say ``magic'' for whatever we did not understand, as a reminder that the problem was still unsolved. ``Complexity'' and ``emergence'' create an illusion of understanding; ``magic'' leaves an honest placeholder.


## honest/sections/say-not-complexity.tex
